<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 2 Focus 48 (respectively.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of Type III hypersensitivity reactions (immune complex deposition) and the specific autoantigen targeted in Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE), specifically anti-dsDNA antibodies and their association with glomerulonephritis. (理解III型过敏反应（免疫复合物沉积）的发病机制以及系统性红斑狼疮（SLE）中特异性自身抗原，特别是抗dsDNA抗体及其与肾小球肾炎的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of worsening facial rash, joint pain (especially in her hands and knees), and fatigue. She reports a history of photosensitivity and recurrent oral ulcers. Her past medical history is significant for Raynaud's phenomenon. On physical examination, she has a malar rash ("butterfly rash") across her cheeks and nose, and her joints are tender and swollen. Laboratory tests reveal elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR), positive antinuclear antibody (ANA) titer (1:160), and positive anti-double-stranded DNA (anti-dsDNA) antibodies. A urine analysis shows proteinuria and hematuria. Renal biopsy reveals subepithelial immune complex deposits ("wire loop" appearance) on electron microscopy, along with mesangial proliferation and endocapillary hypercellularity on light microscopy. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows granular deposition of IgG and C3 along the glomerular basement membrane.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the pathogenesis of the patient's renal findings?

(A) Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies, leading to linear IgG deposition along the GBM.
(B) Formation of immune complexes in situ within the glomerulus, involving glomerular antigens and rheumatoid factor.
(C) Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing anti-dsDNA antibodies, leading to granular IgG and C3 deposition in the subepithelial space.
(D) Complement activation via the alternative pathway due to bacterial endotoxin exposure, resulting in C3 deposition and glomerular damage.
(E) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) targeting podocytes, mediated by anti-podocyte antibodies.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female, classic demographic for SLE.
- **Symptoms:** Malar rash, joint pain, fatigue, photosensitivity, oral ulcers, Raynaud's phenomenon – all highly suggestive of SLE.
- **Lab Findings:** Elevated ESR (inflammation marker), positive ANA (sensitive but non-specific for SLE), positive anti-dsDNA antibodies (highly specific for SLE and associated with lupus nephritis).
- **Urinalysis:** Proteinuria and hematuria indicate kidney involvement (lupus nephritis).
- **Renal Biopsy:**
    - Light Microscopy: Mesangial proliferation, endocapillary hypercellularity – features of proliferative glomerulonephritis.
    - Immunofluorescence: Granular IgG and C3 deposition along the GBM – characteristic of immune complex deposition.
    - Electron Microscopy: Subepithelial "wire loop" deposits – pathognomonic for immune complex deposition in SLE nephritis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the renal findings (glomerulonephritis).

The combination of clinical features (malar rash, arthritis, photosensitivity, etc.), positive ANA and anti-dsDNA antibodies, and renal biopsy findings (granular IgG/C3 deposition, subepithelial "wire loops") strongly points to SLE with lupus nephritis, a classic example of Type III hypersensitivity. The anti-dsDNA antibodies form circulating immune complexes with dsDNA antigens, which then deposit in the glomeruli, activate complement, and cause inflammation and damage.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Type III Hypersensitivity:** Mediated by immune complexes (antigen-antibody complexes). These complexes deposit in tissues (like glomeruli, blood vessels, joints) and activate complement, leading to inflammation, neutrophil recruitment, and tissue damage.
- **SLE:** A systemic autoimmune disease characterized by the production of autoantibodies against various self-antigens, including nuclear components like dsDNA, histones, and Sm proteins.
- **Anti-dsDNA Antibodies:** Highly specific for SLE. They form immune complexes with dsDNA released from apoptotic cells. These complexes deposit in tissues, particularly the kidneys (glomeruli), leading to lupus nephritis.
- **Lupus Nephritis Pathogenesis:**
    1.  **Immune Complex Formation:** Anti-dsDNA antibodies bind to circulating dsDNA antigens.
    2.  **Deposition:** These immune complexes deposit in the subepithelial space of the glomeruli.
    3.  **Complement Activation:** Deposited complexes activate the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C3).
    4.  **Inflammation:** Complement activation generates C3a and C5a (anaphylatoxins), attracting neutrophils and macrophages. C5b-9 (MAC) formation can also cause cell lysis.
    5.  **Tissue Damage:** Neutrophils release proteases and reactive oxygen species, damaging the glomerular basement membrane and podocytes, leading to proteinuria, hematuria, and renal failure.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings in SLE Nephritis:**
    - Light Microscopy: Variable findings depending on the class of nephritis (e.g., mesangial, proliferative, membranous). Often shows mesangial proliferation, endocapillary hypercellularity, and sometimes crescent formation.
    - Immunofluorescence: Granular ("lumpy-bumpy") deposition of IgG, IgM, IgA, C3, and C1q along the glomerular basement membrane and/or in the mesangium.
    - Electron Microscopy: Subepithelial electron-dense deposits ("wire loops"), mesangial deposits, and GBM thickening.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Describes Goodpasture syndrome (anti-GBM disease), a Type II hypersensitivity reaction. Anti-GBM antibodies directly target the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in the GBM, leading to *linear* IgG deposition on immunofluorescence, not granular. This patient has granular deposition and positive anti-dsDNA, ruling out anti-GBM disease.
- (B): Describes a possible mechanism in some forms of glomerulonephritis (e.g., membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis associated with cryoglobulinemia or rheumatoid arthritis), but it's not the primary mechanism in SLE nephritis. SLE nephritis is primarily driven by circulating immune complexes containing anti-dsDNA, not *in situ* complex formation involving rheumatoid factor (which is associated with rheumatoid arthritis, not typically SLE nephritis).
- (D): Describes a mechanism potentially seen in post-infectious glomerulonephritis or certain types of membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN). While complement activation is crucial in SLE nephritis, the primary trigger is immune complex deposition, not direct activation by bacterial endotoxin. The deposition pattern (granular, subepithelial) is also inconsistent with alternative pathway activation alone.
- (E): Describes a potential mechanism in certain autoimmune diseases affecting the glomerulus (e.g., anti-podocyte antibody disease), but it's not the characteristic mechanism of SLE nephritis. SLE nephritis is primarily mediated by immune complex deposition and complement activation, leading to the characteristic biopsy findings described. While podocytes are damaged, the primary initiating event is immune complex deposition, not direct ADCC targeting podocytes.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | SLE Nephritis (Type III)                                  | Anti-GBM Disease (Type II)                               | Post-Infectious GN (Type III)                             | MPGN (Type III)                                        |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mechanism**   | Circulating immune complex deposition (anti-dsDNA)         | Antibody directly targeting GBM (anti-GBM)                 | Circulating immune complex deposition (post-infection)     | Circulating immune complex deposition (various triggers)   |
| **Antibody Specificity** | Anti-dsDNA, anti-Sm, ANA                                  | Anti-GBM (alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen)               | Anti-streptococcal antigens                               | Variable (e.g., cryoglobulins, C3 nephritic factor)       |
| **IF Pattern**          | Granular IgG, C3 (subepithelial, mesangial)                | Linear IgG along GBM                                      | Granular IgG, C3 (subepithelial, mesangial, capillary loops) | Granular IgG, C3 (subendothelial, mesangial, intramembranous) |
| **EM Findings**         | Subepithelial "wire loops", mesangial deposits             | GBM thickening, no deposits                               | Subepithelial "humps"                                     | Subendothelial deposits, GBM splitting                   |
| **Associated Disease**  | Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE)                         | Goodpasture Syndrome                                      | Post-streptococcal infection                               | Hepatitis C, cryoglobulinemia, autoimmune diseases         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
SLE nephritis is a classic example of Type III hypersensitivity mediated by circulating immune complexes containing anti-dsDNA antibodies. The characteristic renal biopsy findings (granular IgG/C3 deposition, subepithelial "wire loops") are key diagnostic features. Differentiate SLE nephritis from Type II hypersensitivity (anti-GBM disease, linear IF) and other Type III reactions (e.g., post-infectious GN, MPGN) based on specific autoantigens, immune complex composition, and deposition patterns.

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 2 Focus 49 (Complement)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation (specifically C5a) in mediating inflammation and its contribution to the pathogenesis of autoimmune diseases like systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), particularly in the context of immune complex deposition and neutrophil recruitment. (理解补体激活（特别是C5a）在介导炎症中的作用，以及它在自身免疫性疾病（如系统性红斑狼疮）发病机制中的贡献，特别是在免疫复合物沉积和中性粒细胞募集的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents with a 6-month history of fatigue, joint pain (especially in the hands and knees), photosensitivity (malar rash appearing after sun exposure), and recurrent oral ulcers. She denies fever, weight loss, or significant respiratory symptoms. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she takes no regular medications. Family history is positive for rheumatoid arthritis in her mother. Physical examination reveals a butterfly-shaped erythematous rash across her cheeks and nose, swollen and tender metacarpophalangeal (MCP) and proximal interphalangeal (PIP) joints, and several small, painless ulcers on her buccal mucosa. Laboratory tests show a positive antinuclear antibody (ANA) titer (1:160), positive anti-dsDNA antibodies, low C3 and C4 complement levels, and elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR). A renal biopsy reveals subepithelial immune complex deposits ("wire loop" appearance) in the glomeruli, accompanied by significant neutrophil infiltration and glomerular damage.

Which of the following complement components or downstream mediators is MOST directly responsible for the neutrophil infiltration observed in the renal biopsy?

(A) C1q, initiating the classical pathway of complement activation.
(B) C3b, opsonizing immune complexes for phagocytosis by macrophages.
(C) C5a, acting as a potent chemoattractant for neutrophils.
(D) C5b-9 (MAC), directly lysing glomerular cells.
(E) Factor B, a component of the alternative pathway lectin pathway.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with classic symptoms of SLE (fatigue, arthralgia, photosensitivity/malar rash, oral ulcers) over 6 months.
- **Family History:** Positive for autoimmune disease (rheumatoid arthritis), increasing suspicion for SLE.
- **Physical Exam:** Confirms SLE features (malar rash, arthritis, oral ulcers).
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Positive ANA and anti-dsDNA: Highly specific for SLE.
    - Low C3 and C4: Indicates complement consumption, common in active SLE due to immune complex formation and activation of the classical pathway.
    - Elevated ESR: Non-specific marker of inflammation.
- **Renal Biopsy:** Subepithelial immune complex deposits ("wire loop" appearance) are characteristic of lupus nephritis (specifically Class IV). Neutrophil infiltration is a key feature of the inflammatory process causing glomerular damage.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mediator MOST directly responsible for the neutrophil infiltration seen in the biopsy. This points towards a chemoattractant involved in the inflammatory response triggered by complement activation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **SLE Pathogenesis:** SLE is an autoimmune disease characterized by the production of autoantibodies (like anti-dsDNA) that form immune complexes. These complexes deposit in various tissues, including the kidneys (glomeruli).
- **Complement Activation:** Immune complex deposition triggers the classical pathway of complement activation. C1q binds to the Fc portion of IgG in the immune complexes. This initiates a cascade leading to the cleavage of C3 into C3a and C3b, and C5 into C5a and C5b.
- **Role of Complement Components:**
    - **C1q:** Initiates the classical pathway. While essential for activation, it doesn't directly cause neutrophil infiltration.
    - **C3b:** Acts as an opsonin, coating immune complexes and pathogens to enhance phagocytosis by macrophages and neutrophils. It also participates in the formation of C5 convertase. While important for clearance, it's not the primary chemoattractant.
    - **C5a:** A potent anaphylatoxin and chemoattractant. It binds to C5a receptors (C5aR) on neutrophils, monocytes, eosinophils, and mast cells. Binding to C5aR on neutrophils triggers chemotaxis (migration towards the source of C5a), degranulation, and increased phagocytic activity. C5a is a major mediator of inflammation in SLE, particularly in lupus nephritis, driving neutrophil recruitment to sites of immune complex deposition.
    - **C5b-9 (Membrane Attack Complex - MAC):** Forms pores in target cell membranes, leading to cell lysis. While MAC can contribute to tissue damage in some autoimmune diseases, its primary role in SLE nephritis is less significant than C5a-mediated inflammation and neutrophil recruitment. Direct lysis of glomerular cells by MAC is not the main mechanism of injury.
    - **Factor B:** A component of the alternative pathway C3 convertase (C3bBb). While the alternative pathway can be activated in SLE, the classical pathway is typically the primary route initiated by immune complexes. Factor B itself is not a chemoattractant.
- **Neutrophil Infiltration:** The presence of neutrophils in the glomeruli (as seen in the biopsy) is a hallmark of active lupus nephritis. This infiltration is driven primarily by chemoattractants released during complement activation, with C5a being the most potent among the complement fragments.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): C1q initiates the classical pathway, which is crucial in SLE pathogenesis due to immune complexes. However, C1q itself does not directly attract neutrophils. Its role is upstream in the cascade.
- (B): C3b is a major opsonin and part of the C5 convertase. It enhances phagocytosis but is not the primary chemoattractant for neutrophils. While important for the overall inflammatory response, C5a is more directly responsible for chemotaxis.
- (D): C5b-9 (MAC) causes cell lysis. While it can contribute to tissue damage, the primary mechanism of glomerular injury in lupus nephritis involves inflammation driven by immune complexes, complement activation fragments (like C5a), and neutrophil infiltration, rather than direct lysis of glomerular cells by MAC.
- (E): Factor B is part of the alternative pathway C3 convertase. While the alternative pathway might contribute, the classical pathway is predominantly activated by immune complexes in SLE. Factor B is not a chemoattractant for neutrophils.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | C1q                                  | C3b                                  | C5a                                  | C5b-9 (MAC)                          | Factor B                               |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Function**         | Initiates Classical Pathway          | Opsonin, C5 Convertase Component     | Chemoattractant, Anaphylatoxin       | Cell Lysis                            | Alternative Pathway C3 Convertase Component |
| **Role in SLE**      | Activates complement cascade         | Enhances phagocytosis, C5 convertase | Recruits neutrophils, inflammation   | Minor role in glomerular damage       | Minor role (Alternative pathway)       |
| **Neutrophil Link**  | Indirect (initiates cascade)         | Indirect (opsonin)                   | **Direct (potent chemoattractant)** | Indirect (potential cell damage)      | Indirect                               |
| **Key Association**  | Immune complex binding (classical)   | Phagocytosis, opsonization           | **Inflammation, neutrophil recruitment** | Cell lysis, membrane damage           | Alternative pathway activation         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The potent chemoattractant C5a, generated during complement activation (especially the classical pathway triggered by immune complexes in SLE), is the primary mediator responsible for recruiting neutrophils to sites of inflammation, such as the glomeruli in lupus nephritis. Understanding the specific functions of complement components is crucial for understanding the pathogenesis of complement-mediated diseases. Differentiating C5a's role as a chemoattractant from C1q's initiation role, C3b's opsonization role, C5b-9's lytic role, and Factor B's alternative pathway role is key.

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 2 Focus 50 (mediated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of Type II hypersensitivity reactions involving autoantibodies targeting cell surface receptors, specifically focusing on Myasthenia Gravis and the role of acetylcholine receptors (AChRs) at the neuromuscular junction. (理解II型过敏反应的发病机制，包括针对细胞表面受体的自身抗体，特别是重症肌无力中针对神经肌肉接头乙酰胆碱受体(AChRs)的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents with progressively worsening muscle weakness, particularly affecting her ocular and bulbar muscles. She reports difficulty chewing, swallowing, and speaking, along with ptosis and diplopia, which worsen with activity and improve with rest. Her symptoms began insidiously over several months. Neurological examination reveals fatigable weakness of extraocular muscles, facial muscles, and proximal limb muscles. Reflexes are normal, and sensory examination is unremarkable. Electromyography (EMG) shows a decremental response on repetitive nerve stimulation. Serum analysis reveals elevated levels of antibodies against the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (AChR). Muscle biopsy shows normal morphology but reveals an increased number of AChRs at the postsynaptic membrane, suggesting an attempt at compensation. Which of the following mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Complement-mediated lysis of motor neurons by antibodies targeting myelin basic protein.
(B) Antibody-mediated cross-linking and internalization (endocytosis) of acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against muscle fibers expressing MHC class I molecules presenting AChR peptides.
(D) Formation of immune complexes containing AChR antibodies that deposit in the glomeruli, causing kidney damage.
(E) Antibody-mediated blockade of the acetylcholine esterase enzyme, leading to accumulation of acetylcholine in the synaptic cleft.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female, typical demographic for autoimmune diseases, including Myasthenia Gravis (MG).
- **Symptoms:** Progressive, fatigable muscle weakness (ocular, bulbar, proximal limbs), worsening with activity, improving with rest. This pattern is highly characteristic of MG due to impaired neuromuscular transmission.
- **Neurological Exam:** Fatigable weakness of specific muscle groups (extraocular, facial, proximal limbs), normal reflexes and sensation. This localizes the problem to the neuromuscular junction, sparing sensory nerves and reflexes.
- **Diagnostic Tests:**
    - EMG: Decremental response on repetitive nerve stimulation confirms impaired neuromuscular transmission, a hallmark of MG.
    - Serum Antibodies: Elevated anti-AChR antibodies are diagnostic for MG.
    - Muscle Biopsy: Increased number of AChRs at the postsynaptic membrane is a compensatory response to receptor loss, further supporting MG.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *mechanism* of pathogenesis.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Myasthenia Gravis (MG):** An autoimmune disease characterized by fluctuating muscle weakness.
- **Pathogenesis:** Primarily mediated by autoantibodies (IgG) directed against components of the postsynaptic membrane at the neuromuscular junction. The most common target is the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (AChR).
- **Mechanism of Antibody Action:**
    - **Antibody Binding:** Anti-AChR antibodies bind to AChRs on the motor endplate.
    - **Complement Activation:** Antibody binding can activate the classical complement pathway, leading to damage of the postsynaptic membrane (membrane attack complex formation).
    - **Receptor Internalization/Degradation:** Antibody cross-linking of AChRs promotes their internalization via endocytosis and subsequent degradation within lysosomes, reducing the number of functional receptors available for acetylcholine binding.
    - **Receptor Blockade:** Some antibodies may directly block the acetylcholine binding site on the receptor, preventing acetylcholine from activating the receptor.
- **Result:** Reduced number of functional AChRs and/or blockade of AChRs leads to decreased efficiency of neuromuscular transmission, resulting in muscle weakness and fatigability.
- **USMLE Relevance:** MG is a classic example of a Type II hypersensitivity reaction mediated by autoantibodies against cell surface receptors. Understanding the specific mechanisms by which these antibodies cause disease is crucial.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This describes a potential mechanism in Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS) or certain forms of autoimmune polyneuropathy, where antibodies target myelin components (like myelin basic protein) or axons, leading to demyelination or axonal damage. GBS typically presents with ascending paralysis and areflexia, not fatigable weakness localized to specific muscle groups. MG targets the neuromuscular junction, not the myelin sheath of motor neurons.
- (C): **Incorrect.** While T-cell mediated cytotoxicity can occur in some autoimmune diseases (e.g., Type 1 diabetes targeting pancreatic beta cells), MG is primarily an antibody-mediated disease. T cells are not the main effector cells causing the destruction or dysfunction of AChRs.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes a potential complication of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) or other diseases involving circulating immune complexes. While MG is an autoimmune disease, the primary pathology is localized to the neuromuscular junction, not systemic immune complex deposition causing glomerulonephritis. Although some MG patients may have other autoimmune conditions, this is not the primary mechanism of MG itself.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Acetylcholinesterase (AChE) is the enzyme that breaks down acetylcholine in the synaptic cleft. Inhibiting AChE (e.g., with drugs like pyridostigmine) *increases* acetylcholine levels and *improves* neuromuscular transmission in MG. Antibodies in MG target the AChR, not AChE. Blocking AChE would alleviate, not cause, the symptoms.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Myasthenia Gravis (MG)                                  | Guillain-Barré Syndrome (GBS)                             | Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM)                          | Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE)                       |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|
| **Immune Mechanism** | Type II Hypersensitivity (Anti-AChR antibodies)         | Autoimmune neuropathy (Anti-ganglioside/myelin antibodies) | Type I Hypersensitivity (T-cell mediated beta-cell lysis) | Type III Hypersensitivity (Immune complex deposition)    |
| **Target Antigen**   | Acetylcholine Receptor (AChR) at NMJ                   | Myelin (MBP, P2), Gangliosides (GM1)                     | Pancreatic beta-cell antigens (e.g., GAD65, IA-2)        | Nuclear antigens (dsDNA, Sm), RBCs, Platelets, WBCs, etc. |
| **Primary Pathology**| Impaired neuromuscular transmission, fatigable weakness | Demyelination/Axonal damage, ascending paralysis, areflexia | Beta-cell destruction, insulin deficiency                | Multi-system inflammation, immune complex deposition     |
| **Key Symptoms**     | Fatigable ocular/bulbar/limb weakness                  | Ascending paralysis, areflexia                            | Polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss                         | Arthritis, rash, nephritis, serositis, cytopenias        |
| **Antibodies**       | Anti-AChR, Anti-MuSK                                   | Anti-GM1, Anti-GD1a, Anti-myelin                          | Anti-GAD65, Anti-IA-2, Anti-insulin                      | Anti-dsDNA, Anti-Sm, ANA                                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Myasthenia Gravis is a classic Type II hypersensitivity reaction where autoantibodies target cell surface receptors (AChRs) at the neuromuscular junction, leading to impaired signal transduction and muscle weakness. The primary mechanisms involve receptor internalization/degradation and complement-mediated damage. Differentiate MG from other neuromuscular disorders like GBS (demyelination), Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (presynaptic calcium channel antibodies), and motor neuron diseases (ALS). Remember that MG is antibody-mediated, not T-cell mediated (like T1DM) or immune complex-mediated (like SLE nephritis).
